--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to a new edition of Nicomachus of Gerasa's Introduction to Arithmetic, establishing the history of previous editions and the need for a more rigorous scholarly approach.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

After **Christian Wechel** first published the arithmetic introduction of Nicomachus of Gerasa <note>A Pythagorean mathematician from the 1st/2nd century AD</note> in Paris in the year **1538**, **Friedrich Ast** in the **17th** year of this century <note>1817</note> added a version to his edition of the *Theology of Arithmetic*. This version was revised according to the Paris example, corrected with the help of manuscript codices from Munich, and provided with broad and wordy annotations. While no one would deny that in this edition many passages—which the first edition had presented fouled by the most absurd errors and corruptions—are offered in a corrected state, nevertheless the skill which Ast applied to restoring the text of Nicomachus to a better condition cannot be judged worthy of praise for either soundness or consistency. For since he cared nothing for the relationship that existed between the codices and the Parisian edition, and considered all the books available to him to be of equal value, it happened that he admitted the readings of each one into the text at his own discretion. Furthermore, he almost entirely neglected codex *m* (238), by far the best of the Munich manuscripts, because it more frequently agreed with the Wechel edition; instead, he followed codex *μ* (76)—which anyone who has inspected it once will agree should be held in the lowest esteem—just as much as the much better codex *S* (482). Nor did Ast err less when he strove to "correct" those opinions which Nicomachus brings forward from the writings of Plato and others according to the original books of those authors; he seems to have been ignorant of the fact that among the ancients, the sayings of writers are very often seen to be cited from memory. Indeed, there is a greater number of passages which he attempted to reduce to the elegance of Platonic speech, even though the consensus of all the codices argued against it.

<summary>The editor critiques Friedrich Ast's 1817 edition of Nicomachus, arguing that Ast lacked a consistent philological method and ignored the best manuscripts in favor of inferior ones.</summary>
<keywords>Nicomachus of Gerasa, Christian Wechel, Friedrich Ast, textual criticism, Munich codices, Plato, arithmetic, history of printing</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Friedrich Ast’s 1817 edition of Nicomachus, specifically his editorial failings, and provides a biographical sketch of Nicomachus of Gerasa to establish his historical context.</meta>

...attempted [to correct the text], he corrupted and spoiled it by this very effort, forgetting that there is a significant difference between Plato and our arithmetician, whom a barbarian region <note>Likely referring to Gerasa in Roman Judea from a classical perspective</note> produced almost five centuries later. *) Nevertheless, Ast was the first to restore the memory of Nicomachus in Germany.

Nor were there lacking those who followed in his footsteps. For in a scholarly pamphlet for the episcopal school of **Zeitz** <note>Latin: *Cizensis*</note> in the 18th year of this century <note>1818</note>, **Gottfried Müller** added "*the ninth part of the report and review of the manuscript codices which are preserved in the library of the Bishopric of Naumburg-Zeitz*" <note>original: "particulam nonam notitiae et recensionis codicum mss. qui in bibliotheca episcopatus Numburgo-Cizensis asseruantur"</note> in which he judged the text of the Zeitz codex—which "since it had almost all good qualities in common with the Munich codices, and even some unique to itself"—worthy "to be brought into the light and received into the number of already known manuscript codices," comparing its readings with the Astian edition...

---

<insert>*) Lest I seem to remain entirely silent regarding the life and age of Nicomachus, I will set forth here what can be said with certainty. Our author was born in the city of **Gerasa** in Judea, located near Bostra, as testified by a note from **John Philoponus** <note>A 6th-century scholar and commentator</note> regarding the title of the first book of Nicomachus’s arithmetic; but as to when he lived, we can speak with greater frequency. These testimonies prove to us that he flourished around the 100th year after the birth of Christ: since Nicomachus praises the mathematician **Thrasyllus** (<term>Manual of Harmonics</term> <note>Latin: *enchiridion harmon.*</note> I p. 24: "and we accurately acquired the Pythagorean division of the canon <note>The 'canon' refers to the monochord, a tool for measuring musical intervals</note> according to the teacher's intention, completed not as Eratosthenes or Thrasyllus misunderstood it, but as Timaeus of Locri..." etc. <note>original Greek: καὶ προσκτησάμεθα τὴν Πυθαγορικὸν κανόνος κατατομὴν ἀκριβῶς κατὰ τὸ βούλημα τοῦ διδασκάλου συντετελεσμένων οὐχ ὡς Ἐρατοσθένης παρήκουον ἢ Θράσυλλος, ἀλλ᾽ ὡς ὁ Δονχὸς Τίμαιος κτλ.</note>), whom **Tacitus** and **Suetonius** record as having lived in the times of Tiberius (Suet. Tib. XIV. — Tac. Ann. VI, 20 and 22. — cf. Juvenal, Satire VI, 576: *the numbers of Thrasyllus*. — Dio Cassius LV, 11), yet he himself is unknown to **Theon of Smyrna**, who frequently mentions Thrasyllus and almost all earlier mathematical writers (in *On Music* II p. 74; XXXV p. 137; XXXVI p. 145, Bullialdus edition); furthermore, **Cassiodorus** relates (in his book *On Arithmetic*, near the end) that **Apuleius of Madaura**, who is known to have written during the time of the Antonines <note>The 2nd-century Roman dynasty including Marcus Aurelius</note>, "offered Nicomachus’s arithmetic to the Romans to be read, translated into the Latin language." — Besides the introduction to arithmetic, there exists Nicomachus’s <term>Manual of Harmonics</term> <note>Greek: ἐγχειρίδιον ἁρμονικῆς</note>, which **I. Meursius** (Leiden, 1616) and **M. Meibomius** (Amsterdam, 1652) edited. — A fragment of Nicomachus concerning **Pythagoras** is found in **Iamblichus**, *On the Life of Pythagoras*, chapter 35, p. 492 (Kiessling edition). — The so-called <term>Theological Principles of Arithmetic</term> <note>Latin: *Theologumena Arithmetica*</note> are falsely attributed to Nicomachus by **Photius** <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople and scholar</note> (codex 187, p. 142, Beck edition), who distinguishes our *Introduction* from the arithmetic of Nicomachus itself.</insert>

<summary>This page details the 19th-century editorial history of Nicomachus's work, specifically Gottfried Müller's analysis of the Zeitz codex, and provides a scholarly biography placing Nicomachus in the late 1st to early 2nd century AD based on historical witnesses like Apuleius and Thrasyllus.</summary>
<keywords>Nicomachus of Gerasa, Friedrich Ast, Gottfried Müller, Zeitz codex, Thrasyllus, Apuleius of Madaura, Manual of Harmonics, history of mathematics, manuscript tradition</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the editorial work on Nicomachus's *Introduction to Arithmetic*, moving from Gottfried Müller’s early 19th-century study of the Zeitz manuscript to Karl Nobbe’s 1828 specimen, and finally to the current editor's discovery of the superior 10th-century Göttingen manuscript.</meta>

# PREFACE

instituted this comparison. Although Müller did not lack diligence in his effort, his lack of expertise <note>Latin: *imperitia*</note> was so great that he completely missed the relationship between the manuscript books—specifically between the Zeitz manuscript and the Munich 76 ($\mu$). Furthermore, it even seemed "**likely, even credible**" to him "**that the scribe to whom that codex is owed had compared several copies and from these transferred into his own copy the readings which he himself approved.**" Nevertheless, he correctly perceived that the Zeitz codex (C) was written by a learned Greek man, and that many passages which appear corrupted in the Wechel edition and in Ast’s manuscripts are provided in a corrected and emended form in the Zeitz book.

This review of the Zeitz codex by Müller was followed ten years later by a "*specimen of Nicomachus's arithmetic*" which **Karl Nobbe** of Leipzig published in the year 1828, having brought into the comparison the Nuremberg and Wolfenbüttel manuscripts, which Ast had not known. Since Nobbe was acquainted with neither the Zeitz manuscript nor Müller’s pamphlet—which had been printed and offered for sale in Leipzig—he considered it sufficient, in the first three chapters of the first book (which were the only ones he published), to add the scholia <note>Ancient or medieval marginal commentaries</note> of his own manuscripts and the variations in the text to Ast’s reading. Nor did he perceive much benefit from the preparation of a certain anonymous scholar from Halle, which he had received for review. (As for the work Nobbe published four years ago, having "**fallen back upon that stored-away pamphlet of a certain foreign philologist**," the scholia of manuscripts *N* and *Γ* for the first book are of too little value to provide any use for emending the arithmetic of Nicomachus.)

This was the research material available when, before I prepared to publish the commentary of **John Philoponus**, I personally revised Nicomachus once again when the opportunity arose (in the program of the **Wetzlar Gymnasium** in the year 1862), following primarily the excellence of the Zeitz manuscript (C) in its variation of readings. But it could not be long before I realized that even this book was far removed from the text of the archetype <note>The original or most ancient source manuscript</note>. For, by the benevolence of the distinguished **Hermann Sauppe**, I was fortunate enough to be allowed to use that **Göttingen codex**—of which I spoke more at length in the preface to my edition of the scholia of John Philoponus. This manuscript is not only the most ancient—it dates to the tenth century, whereas all the others were written after the 13th century—but it is also superior to all others by such a vast distance that, as far as possible, I have preferred it above...

<summary>The editor critiques previous scholars Gottfried Müller and Karl Nobbe for their flawed or incomplete use of manuscripts, before announcing his discovery of a 10th-century Göttingen manuscript that is far superior to all other known copies of Nicomachus.</summary>

<keywords>Gottfried Müller, Karl Nobbe, Zeitz manuscript, Munich manuscript, Wolfenbüttel manuscript, Nuremberg manuscript, Hermann Sauppe, Göttingen manuscript, John Philoponus, textual criticism, manuscript tradition</keywords>

# VI PREFACE

<insert>London, Harleian 6295, ff 124–126</insert>

it immediately became apparent that others had to be employed by me in this new edition of Nicomachus. Nor, when I compared the Göttingen manuscript with the Paris edition and the other books <note>Latin: *libris*; here meaning manuscript volumes</note> available to me, could it escape my notice that all the manuscripts of Nicomachus fall into two distinct classes <note>Latin: *genera*</note>, which I believe I can present most clearly to the reader in this diagram:

->**Archetype**<-
---
<image-desc>A stemma diagram showing the relationship between manuscript groups. From the "archetype" at the top, a horizontal line branches into two groups: "Class I" and "Class II". A bracket under "Class I" groups the manuscripts G, m, and P. A bracket under "Class II" groups the manuscripts C, μ, and G₂SH[N]Γ.</image-desc>

The index of books is as follows:

**G** = The Göttingen codex, from the tenth century, written on parchment in quarto <term>a book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</term>, consisting of 266 leaves. It is written most elegantly, with titles painted in red lead <note>Latin: *minio*</note>; it contains Nicomachus’s *Introduction* along with the commentary of John Philoponus, to which a scholarly note <note>scholion</note> by Theodore Protocensor has been added. (Regarding the history of this manuscript, see the first page of my preface to the edition of John Philoponus.)
**G₂** = A second hand <note>Latin: *manus altera*</note>, much more recent, which corrected the writing of the codex according to a copy of the second class.
**m** = Munich codex 238, from the 14th century, written on paper in quarto, consisting of 19 leaves. It was produced by Boemundus Canonicus and contains chapters 1 through 19 of the first book, "up to the whole" <note>Greek: *πρὸς τῷ ὅλῳ*</note>. (See Aretin, *Contributions to History and Literature*, Vol. IV, p. 586, 1805.)
**P** = The Wechel edition, printed in Paris in 1538 in quarto. Errors by the typesetter indicate that this edition was transcribed from a reasonably old Class I book by someone who was not very skilled. For instance, in **G**, the letters *beta* (β) and *kappa* (κ) have almost the same form (u) when placed before the letter *delta* (δ); consequently, in **P**, *beta* is often substituted for *kappa* and vice-versa. Thus, one might read "seventh" <note>Greek: *ἔκδομος*, a misspelling of *ἕβδομος*</note> and so on. Friedrich Matthi, a most learned man who inspected the books, informed me that fourteen manuscripts of Nicomachus are preserved in the Imperial Library—all of more recent date—but none of them match the Wechel edition.
**C** = The Zeitz codex, written at the end of the 14th or beginning of the 15th century, on paper in the largest format <note>folio</note>. It contains Nicomachus on leaf 33, as well as a recent version of John Philoponus's notes...

<insert>from Leiden, University Library, Voss. Gr. Q 23</insert>

<vocab>Nicomachus, Göttingen codex, John Philoponus, Theodore Protocensor, second hand, Munich codex 238, Boemundus Canonicus, Wechel edition, Friedrich Matthi, Zeitz codex</vocab>

He provides a detailed catalog of the primary sources used for this edition, noting their physical characteristics and scribal histories.</summary>

# VII PREFACE

<insert>add: Escorial Σ - II - 15, leaves 160–171</insert>

[...the second] version and those problems which have been added to this edition. <note>original Latin: "sionem alteram et ea problemata quae huic adiecta sunt editioni"</note> (*cf.* Müller's notice and review of the Zeitz manuscripts, part IX, Leipzig 1818, 8vo; and the Preface to John Philoponus, p. 1.)
**C₂** = a second hand.
**$\mu$** = Munich codex 76, 16th century, paper, of the largest size, written with the greatest lack of care and negligence, regarding which *cf.* Aretin II, p. 41 (1804). — It seems very likely to me that this book was copied from codex **C**, as it presents its errors joined with its own.
**S** = Munich codex 482, 13th or 14th century, by a different hand, cotton paper <term>Latin: *bombycinus*; a type of paper made from cotton or silk fibers rather than wood or rags</term> in quarto, 252 leaves; from leaf 96 it contains Nicomachus, with many interlinear and marginal notes. (*cf.* Aretin VIII, p. 521, 1805 *)
**S₂** = a second hand.
**H** = Hamburg codex, 16th century, paper, largest size, handed over by Lucas Holstenius to the Hamburg library, most diligently transcribed from a Roman or Toulouse book. (*cf.* *Life of the Scholar Lucas Holstenius*, Hamburg 1723, pp. 7, 44, 94.)
[**N** = Nuremberg codex, late 14th or early 15th century, cotton paper in quarto, regarding which see Nobbe's specimen, 1828, p. 5, and the same author's edition of the scholia <note>ancient scholarly commentaries</note>, 1862, p. 3. — I was not granted the opportunity of inspecting the book itself.]
**Γ** = Wolfenbüttel codex, 15th or 16th century, paper, largest size, most negligently written and not worthy of use by me except in a few places. (*cf.* Nobbe's specimen, p. 6; edition of the scholia, p. 3.)
to which is added
**Ast.** = Ast’s edition, Leipzig 1817, 8vo, which I mentioned above.
Hardly of less importance than the codices themselves were those things which the ancient interpreters and other Greek mathematical writers contributed to explaining Nicomachus's books; their designations are as follows:
**Iambl.** = Iamblichus of Chalcis . . . *On the Arithmetical Introduction of Nicomachus* . . . Fourth Book. <note>original Greek: Ἰαμβλίχου Χαλκιδέως . . . περὶ τῆς Νικομάχου ἀριθμητικῆς εἰσαγωγῆς . . . λόγος τέταρτος</note>

VIII
# PREFACE

Edited by Samuel Tennulius. Deventer, 1668 <note>original: CIƆIƆCLXVIII</note> in quarto <term>a book size where the paper sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</term>.
(Including Joachim Camerarius’s explanation of Nicomachus. Deventer, 1667 in quarto.)

**Io. Phil.** = *John Philoponus the Grammarian of Alexandria’s Commentary on the First Book of Nicomachus’s Arithmetic.* <note>original Greek: Ἰωάννου γραμματικοῦ Ἀλεξανδρέως τοῦ Φιλοπόνου εἰς τὸ πρῶτον τῆς Νικομάχου ἀριθμητικῆς</note> First edited by Richard Hoche. Leipzig, 1864 in quarto. — Since the Johannine scholia <term>ancient or medieval marginal commentaries</term> on the second book of Nicomachus have not yet been edited, I have inspected the manuscripts themselves:
**Io. Phil. I** = The first version of John's scholia as provided by manuscripts *G* and *H*.
**Io. Phil. II** = The second version found in manuscript *C*.

**Soter.** = *Scholia of the philosopher Lord Soterichus on the passage in the second book of the Arithmetic: "it is useful both for Plato's generation of the soul and for all harmonic intervals"* <note>original Greek: Τοῦ κυρίου Σωτηρίχου τοῦ φιλοσόφου σχόλια εἰς τὸ ῥητὸν τοῦ δευτέρου βιβλίου τῆς ἀριθμητικῆς τό· χρησιμεύει δὲ εἴς τε τὴν Πλάτωνος ψυχογονίαν καὶ εἰς τὰ ἁρμονικὰ διαστήματα πάντα</note> [II, 2, 3]. Unpublished, found in manuscript *H*.

**Theodor.** = *Scholium of Theodore the Protocensor* <note>original Greek: Σχόλιον Θεοδώρου Πρωτοκένσωρος; "Protocensor" was a high-ranking Byzantine administrative title</note> in manuscript *G*, edited by me in the preface to the first volume of John Philoponus’s scholia, page XIV.

**Io. Pedias.** = *John Pediasimus’s Synopsis of the Measurement and Division of the Earth,* <note>original Greek: Ἰωάννου τοῦ Πεδιασήμου σύνοψις μετρήσεως καὶ μερισμοῦ γῆς</note> hitherto unpublished, which Friedlein of Ansbach discussed in *Jahn’s New Annals* XCII, pages 366–83 (1865). Friedlein, with his characteristic kindness, informed me that besides those passages he described there (pp. 371, 372), he found no others in the Munich manuscripts of John Pediasimus that pertained to Nicomachus’s arithmetic.

**Schol. NΓ** = Scholia of the Nuremberg and Wolfenbüttel <note>referred to by the Greek letter Gamma (Γ)</note> manuscripts on the first book, which Karl Nobbe edited at Leipzig in 1828 (in a sample of Nicomachus's arithmetic) and in 1862, octavo.

**Schol. C** = Scholia of the Zeitz manuscript <note>original: Cizensis</note> for Book II, chapters 26 and 27, edited by me in a school pamphlet at Wetzlar, 1863, quarto.

**Boëth.** = *The Arithmetic of Saint Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius, divided into two books, declared with an added commentary touching upon the mystical application of numbers.* <note>original Latin: "Diui Anitii Manlii Seuerini Boëtii (!) Arithmetica duobus discreta libris adiecto commentario mysticam numerorum applicationem perstringente declarata"</note> Sold at the shop of Simon de Colines opposite the School of Decrees. Paris, 1521. Folio <term>the largest standard book size, made from folding a sheet of paper once</term>.

PREFACE. IX

to which are added, besides the books of Euclid’s *Elements*:
**Theon** = *Theon of Smyrna... regarding mathematical things useful for reading Plato.* <note>original Greek: Θέωνος Σμυρναίου . . . τῶν κατὰ ἀριθμητικὴν χρησίμων εἰς τὴν τοῦ Πλάτωνος ἀνάγνωσιν</note>
With the Latin translation of Ismael Boulliau <note>Bullialdi</note>, edited by J. J. de Gelder. Leiden, 1827 in octavo <term>a book size where the paper sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves</term>.
**Psell.** = *Michael Constantine Psellus’s Compendium on the four mathematical sciences: arithmetic, etc.* <note>original Greek: Μιχ. Κωνστ. Ψέλλου σύνταγμα εἰς τὰς τέσσαρας μαθηματικὰς ἐπιστήμας, ἀριθμητικὴν κτλ.</note> Edited by Wilhelm Holtzmann <note>G. Xylander</note>. Basel, 1556 in folio <term>a large book size made by folding a sheet of paper once</term>.

The things that Cassiodorus and Martianus Capella (in the seventh book of *The Marriage of Philology and Mercury*) wrote concerning arithmetic are of no importance for correcting or interpreting Nicomachus’s work.

While this collection of scholarly tools <term>the "apparatus," or set of resources used for the edition</term> should not be considered small, it is far from being perfect. For there still lie hidden in the dust of libraries not only those commentaries <term>scholia</term> that Asclepius of Tralles wrote on Nicomachus (cf. Fabricius, *Greek Library* volume IV, p. 5. — C. Miller, *Catalogue of Greek Manuscripts of the Library of the Escorial*, No. 248. — on the condition of the commentaries of Asclepius and John Philoponus in manuscript μ, see Aretin II, p. 41 following, 1804; VIII, p. 521 following, 1805.), but also the little book in which that most learned Arab, Thâbet ibn Qurra Abu l’Hassan, interpreted Nicomachus’s *Introduction* in the ninth century; someone to bring it into the light <note>i.e., to publish it</note> has not yet appeared. (cf. Casiri, *Arabic-Hispanic Library of the Escorial*, Vol. I, p. 390. — Nesselmann, *History of Algebra among the Greeks*, p. 43 and 222.)

I have already said above that I followed the Göttingen manuscript <note>Codex Gottingensis, siglum G</note> in this edition above all others; it was not my plan to include the entire set of variant readings from the other books, yet there will not be much you lack from the Paris edition and the manuscripts *m, C, S, H*. However, for chapters I–III of the first book, I have subjoined a full list of reading discrepancies so that the relationship between the manuscripts might be apparent to the reader. Nor is there anyone who would find fault with me for not departing from the spelling style found in manuscript *G* unless moved by serious reasons? Therefore I wrote *isos* <note>original: ἴσος; meaning "equal"</note>\*), *enas* <note>original: ἐνάς; meaning "unit"</note>, *enatos* <note>original: ἔνατος; meaning "ninth"</note> (*ennas* <note>original: ἐννάς</note> in manuscript *P*, *enneas* <note>original: ἐννεάς</note> in more recent manuscripts: — B. Hase erred in Henri Estienne’s *Thesaurus* under the word *enas*),

---

<note>\*) in manuscript *C*, *isos* <note>original: ἴσος</note> is read everywhere; this spelling should be adopted in the mathematical problems.</note>

# PREFACE.

X

etc. Nor did I correct those passages in which, in **Manuscript G**, one reads *dnein* <note>original: δνεῖν</note>, *aiei* <note>original: αἰεί</note>, or *ean* <note>original: ἐάν</note> instead of *an* <note>original: ὧν</note>, or those where the conjunctions *ean* [if] and *hotan* [whenever] <note>original: ἐάν and ὅταν</note> are found joined with the indicative or optative moods according to the custom of later Greek; I did not adopt terms which are not read in other authors, such as *dios* [double], *migma* [mixture], *kybos* [cube] <note>original: διός, μίγμα, κῦβος</note>, etc. — those elements which I either adopted from inferior books or wrote based on <term>conjecture</term> <note>an educated guess by the editor to restore a damaged text</note> are expressed in small letters, while those which seem as though they should be deleted are enclosed in brackets.

A few things must be added regarding the notation of numbers. In **Manuscript G**, the <term>cardinal numbers</term> <note>counting numbers like one, two, three</note> are signified either by plain common letters or by letters with a straight line written above them: α or ᾱ, β or β̅ (appearing as αʹ, βʹ in the edition by P). An oblique line (αʹ, βʹ) <note>a "prime" symbol</note> is never added. Nor did I find this now-customary numerical mark in the other manuscripts I inspected, except in the very recent ones **Hμ** (and rarely in **m**, which more often follows the method of manuscripts **CS**); manuscripts **CS** exhibit either no sign or this sign: ⎴. Individual thousands (<term>that is, within the myriads</term> <note>original Greek: τουτέστιν ἐντὸς μυριάδων</note> I, 16, 3; cf. Hultsch, *Prolegomena to the Metrological Writers* I, 173) are marked in more recent manuscripts with an oblique line placed below, whereas **Manuscript G** omits this mark almost everywhere. I therefore had no reason to hesitate in returning to the method I followed in editing the commentaries <term>scholia</term> of John Philoponus, signifying cardinal numbers from 1 to 900 with plain letters, and prefixing an oblique line below for the numbers of thousands (α, β, . . . ω, ⎴ — α, β, . . . ϑ).

The <term>ordinal numbers</term> <note>numbers showing order, like first, second, third</note>, as we call them, are signified in **Manuscript G** either by the full word without any abbreviation (<term>tenth</term> <note>original: δέκατος</note>), by the same plain letters used for cardinal numbers (ι = <term>tenth</term>), or by letters distinguished by a straight line with the ending of the word written above: ιōˢ, κδ̊̊, γ̊̊, λα̊ etc. The remaining manuscripts follow a similar method; for instance, **m CS** write them thus: β̊̊, λα̊̊, ῦ̊̊ etc. In the same way, <term>multiplicative numbers</term> <note>numbers like "fourfold" or "four times"</note> are written as: <term>four times</term> <note>original: τετράκις</note> = δ (**G**) = δ̅̊̊ˢ (**G**) = δ̅̊̊ˢ (**CS**) = δ̊̊ˢ (**CS**). As for the notation Friedrich Hultsch employs (cf. *Metrological Writers* I, preface p. 174), where δ̊̊ = <term>fourth</term> <note>original: τέταρτον</note>, I have found this nowhere, nor do I approve of this sign; for while that most learned man rightly advised that two lines drawn obliquely from left to right are placed to represent the syllable *-on* <note>original: ὄν</note>—as in τ̊̊ = <term>the</term> <note>original: τόν</note> or περιττ̊̊ = <term>odd</term> <note>original: περιττόν</note>—it nevertheless escaped him that for the syllable *-on* not distinguished by an accent, a single small line is the abbreviation: τριτ̊̊ <note>likely representing τριττόν, "triple" or "third"</note>

<summary>The editor describes his philological methods, explaining why he preserved certain "later Greek" spellings found in Manuscript G and detailing the various ways manuscripts represent cardinal, ordinal, and multiplicative numbers.</summary>
<keywords>paleography, manuscript G, Greek numerals, cardinal numbers, ordinal numbers, Friedrich Hultsch, orthography, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

# PREFACE. XI

...[the symbol] = "third" <note>original: τρίτον</note>, [the symbol] = "even" <note>original: ἄρτιον</note>; however, by two lines drawn from right to left, the ending *-ein* is noted: [the symbol] = "to say" <note>original: εἰπεῖν</note>. Likewise, the symbol β''—which Hultsch wishes to be "second" or "two-fold" <note>original: δεύτερον or δύοστον</note>—should actually be "of two" <note>original: δυεῖν</note>. For these reasons, I believe one should write "3rd," "4th," "5 times" <note>original: γ^{os}, δ^{ov}, ε^{κις}</note>, etc. From this method of writing, we derive a benefit that is by no means slight: we can also signify those <term>fractions</term> <note>parts of a whole</note> whose numerator is a multiple using two numerical signs: 27/64 = twenty-seven sixty-fourths <note>original: ἑπτακαὶεἰκοσι ἑξηκοστοτέταρτα</note> (= a third and a twelfth and a one-hundred-ninety-second <note>original: τρίτον καὶ δωδέκατον καὶ ἑκατοστοενενηκοστοδεύτερον</note>. See Problem 6: 27/64 = 1/3 + 1/12 + 1/192). The sign **G** which I have used in the problems for "half" <note>original: ἥμισυ</note> is taken from the **Zeitz Manuscript** <note>original: cod. Cizensi</note>.

Regarding the problems of **Manuscript C**, which I first published three years ago in the annals of the **Wetzlar Gymnasium** <note>a prestigious secondary school in Germany where Hoche taught</note>, I have judged them not unworthy of being appended to this edition of Nicomachus’s *Arithmetic*. I have nothing more to add concerning them; nor do I have anything to put forward regarding their authors beyond what Thomas Reinesius—a former owner of the manuscript (see the preface to John Philoponus, page 1), whose notes I have added—and Fabricius have already provided in the *Greek Library* <note>original: Bibliotheca Graeca</note>. Indeed, I believe no one will doubt that the first problem cannot be correctly attributed to Diogenes the Cynic <note>a famous ancient Greek philosopher known for living in a tub and challenging social norms</note>.

->Written at Wesel, on the first of July, 1866.<-

->**Richard Hoche.**<-

The ancients and the first to systematize scientific knowledge <margin>I</margin> under the leadership of Pythagoras defined "philosophy" as the "love of wisdom," <note>original: philosophia as philia sophias</note> as the very name implies. For before Pythagoras, everyone was called "wise" <note>original: sophos</note> by a vague title, just as a carpenter, a cobbler, a pilot, and in general anyone skilled in some art or craft was so named. But Pythagoras—

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the opening argument of Nicomachus’s "Introduction to Arithmetic." On the previous page, Nicomachus explained that Pythagoras was the first to use the term "philosophy." This page defines what "wisdom" and "being" truly mean in the Pythagorean tradition, distinguishing between eternal realities and the changing material world.</meta>

-># NICOMACHUS OF GERASA<-

...having narrowed the application of the name from all things to the knowledge and grasp of **that which truly exists** <term>Being: *to on* in Greek; in this context, it refers to things that are eternal and unchanging, rather than physical objects</term>, and having specifically called the knowledge of the truth found in this "wisdom," he naturally termed the desire and pursuit of this "philosophy," as if to say "the longing for wisdom." <margin>2</margin> He is more worthy of credit than those who define the term differently, insofar as he clarifies the concept of both the specific name and the thing itself. He defined this **wisdom** as the "knowledge of the truth in the things that *are*," <note>original: *tōn ontōn*, the beings</note> believing "knowledge" to be a firm and unmovable grasp of the subject matter, and "beings" to be those things which remain the same and in the same state forever in the universe, never departing from their existence even for a moment. These would be the **immaterial things** <term>Immaterial: *ayla*, literally "without matter"</term>, by participation in which every other thing in the category of objects that share the same name is said to exist and be "this or that" specific thing. <margin>3</margin> For bodily and material things are surely in a state of perpetual flux and constant change, imitating the nature and property of the original, eternal **matter** <note>original: *hylē*</note> and substance; for that matter was, through and through, mutable and subject to alteration. But the things perceived surrounding matter or alongside it are **incorporeal** <note>original: *asōmata*</note>, such as qualities, quantities, configurations, mo— <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *metabolai* (changes) or *metousiai* (participations)</note>

"named"] "called" in $N, \Gamma$ — "desire for wisdom"] "longing for wisdom" in $S, H, N, \Gamma$; "desire" in Iamblichus page 5; "longing" ibid. page 10. — 6. ### INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

<margin>p 4</margin>
<margin>5</margin>
...magnitudes, smallnesses, equalities, relations, activities, dispositions, places, times—simply everything by which the contents of each body are encompassed. In themselves, these exist as motionless and unchangeable, but **by accident** <term>By accident: *symbebēkotōs*; a philosophical term meaning a property that is not part of the essential nature of a thing but happens to belong to it</term> they participate in and share in the experiences affecting the underlying body. It is of such things specifically that **wisdom** is the science; but by association, it is also the science of those things which participate in them—namely, bodies.

<margin>II 10</margin>
**II.** But those former things are by nature immaterial, eternal, and without end. They continue to be the same and unchanging through all time, persisting in their own **essence** <term>Essence: *ousia*; the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing</term>. Each of them is rightly said to be "that which exists." However, things involved in **becoming** and **decay** <note>original: *genesis kai phthora*; the natural cycle of birth and death/disintegration</note>, as well as in growth, shrinking, and every kind of change and participation, appear to be in constant flux. <margin>15</margin> They are called "beings" by the same name as those eternal realities, insofar as they participate in them; yet by their own nature, they are not "truly existing" things. For not even for the briefest moment do they remain the same, but they are always passing away, changing in all sorts of ways according to the account in Plato's *Timaeus*, who says: "What is that which is always **being**... <margin>2</margin>

<summary>Nicomachus distinguishes between eternal, immaterial "beings" (like mathematical qualities) and the ever-changing material world of "becoming." He asserts that true wisdom is the study of these unchanging realities, referencing Plato’s Timaeus to support the idea that physical objects are in a state of constant flux.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the distinction between eternal "beings" and the changing world of physical matter. On the previous page, Nicomachus began defining "wisdom" as the knowledge of true reality. Here, he quotes Plato’s *Timaeus* to distinguish between things that are eternal and things that are merely "becoming," eventually leading into the classification of quantity into "magnitudes" and "multitudes."</meta>

### NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

...and has no beginning, and what that is which is always becoming, but never really is? <note>Nicomachus is quoting Plato’s *Timaeus* (27d), a foundational dialogue for ancient science. Plato asks: what is eternal and what is temporary?</note> The former is graspable by the intellect through reason, being always the same and unchanging; the latter, on the other hand, is the object of opinion accompanied by unreasoning sensation, being born and perishing, but <margin>3</margin> never truly existing. It is therefore reasonable and most necessary—if we desire the appropriate and fitting end for a human being, which is the **good life** <note>original: *eu-zōia*; living well or flourishing, the goal of ancient ethics</note> (and this is achieved through philosophy alone, and by nothing else; and philosophy, as I said, is the **desire for wisdom** <note>original: *sophias orexis*; a literal translation of the word "philosophy"</note>, and wisdom is the knowledge of the truth in things that exist; and of things that exist, some are called so in the proper sense, and others by the same name)—to accurately divide and distinguish the properties that belong <margin>4</margin> to existing things. Now, of those things which exist—both those in the proper sense and those so-called by the same name, which is to say both the **intelligible** <term>Intelligible: *noēta*; things known by the mind or intellect rather than the senses</term> and the **sensible** <term>Sensible: *aisthēta*; things perceived through the five senses</term>—some are united and interconnected, such as an animal, a world, a tree, and similar things, which are properly and specifically called **magnitudes** <note>original: *megethē*; continuous quantities like a line or a solid body</note>; others are divided and placed side by side, as if in a pile, which are called **multitudes** <note>original: *plēthē*; discrete quantities like a group of individual items</note>, such as a flock, a crowd, a heap, a chorus, and <margin>5</margin> the like. Therefore, we must consider wisdom to be the science of these two kinds. But since every multitude and every magnitude is by its own nature necessarily infinite...

<summary>Nicomachus defines philosophy as the pursuit of wisdom and categorizes all objects of study into two types: continuous "magnitudes" (like trees or the world) and discrete "multitudes" (like flocks or heaps).</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, wisdom, philosophy, magnitude, multitude, intelligible vs. sensible, ethics, ontology</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuity note: On the previous page, Nicomachus began a philosophical distinction between "intelligible" things (which are eternal and unchanging) and "sensible" things (which are physical and constantly changing). He concluded by noting that science must deal with things that have a definite nature, leading into this page's discussion on the limits of "multitude" and "magnitude."</meta>

# INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...exists. (For while **multitude** <term>Multitude: *plēthos*; a collection of discrete units, like a pile of stones or a group of people</term>, beginning from a definite root, never ceases its forward progress; **magnitude** <term>Magnitude: *megethos*; a continuous whole, like a line, a surface, or a solid body</term>, on the other hand, being divided from a <margin>5</margin> definite wholeness, can in no way stop its cutting, but for these reasons proceeds toward infinity). Since sciences are by all means sciences of things that are limited, and never of things that are infinite, it is clearly evident that a science could never be established concerning either magnitude or multitude in their simple, absolute forms (for each of these is indefinite in itself: multitude in the direction of the "more," and magnitude <margin>10</margin> in the direction of the "less"), but rather about something defined and separated from both: from multitude, the science concerns **quantity** <term>Quantity: *poson*; refers to "how many" things there are—the domain of numbers</term>, and from magnitude, it concerns **size** <term>Size: *pēlikon*; refers to "how much" there is—the domain of measurement and geometry</term>.

---

### III

Again, starting from the beginning: since of **quantity**, <margin>III</margin> one kind is viewed **in itself** <note>original: *kath' heauto*; independent or absolute quantity</note>, having no relationship to another thing—such as the <margin>15</margin> **even**, the **odd**, the **perfect** <note>A number equal to the sum of its divisors, like 6 (1+2+3)</note>, and similar things—while the other kind already exists in some relation to another thing and is conceived together with its relationship to something else—such as the **double**, the **greater**, the **lesser**, the **half**, the **one-and-a-half** <note>original: *hēmiolion*; the ratio of 3 to 2</note>, the **one-and-a-third** <note>original: *epitriton*; the ratio of 4 to 3</note>, and similar things—it is clear then that two scientific methods will take hold of [these matters]...

---

<summary>Nicomachus explains that science cannot study infinite things, so it must focus on "quantity" (numbers) and "size" (geometry) which are limited. He further divides quantity into two types: absolute (like even/odd numbers) and relative (like ratios).</summary>
<keywords>multitude vs magnitude, quantity vs size, absolute quantity, relative quantity, infinite vs limited, ratios, arithmetic fundamentals</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: On the previous page, Nicomachus established the distinction between "quantity" (how many) and "size" (how much). He had just begun to categorize types of quantity, such as the even, the odd, and the perfect. This page continues by assigning specific sciences to these categories, effectively defining what later became known as the Quadrivium.</meta>

# 6 NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

...will clarify the whole inquiry concerning **quantity**: **Arithmetic** <term>Arithmetic: *arithmētikē*; here meaning the theoretical study of numbers and their properties, rather than simple calculation</term> deals with quantity viewed **in itself**, while **Music** <term>Music: *mousikē*; specifically "harmonics" or the study of numerical ratios that create musical intervals</term> <margin>2</margin> deals with quantity viewed **in relation to another**. Again, since **size** is of two kinds—one part being in stability and at rest, and the other in motion and revolution—two other sciences in the same way will accurately define size: **Geometry** deals with that which is remaining and at rest, while **Spherics** <term>Spherics: *sphairikē*; the study of the motion of the celestial spheres, known today as astronomy</term> deals with that which is moving and revolving.

<margin>3</margin> Therefore, without these sciences, it is not possible to accurately define the "forms of being," nor to find the truth in existing things—the knowledge of which is **Wisdom**. It is also evident that without these, one cannot even correctly practice philosophy. For just as painting contributes to the manual arts for the sake of correct observation, so lines, numbers, harmonic intervals, and the revolutions of circles have a cooperative role toward the learning of wise discourses, as **Androcydes the Pythagorean** <note>original: *Androkydēs ho Pythagorikos*; a 4th-century BCE follower of Pythagoras known for symbolic interpretations of the master's teachings</note> says.

<margin>4</margin> But also **Archytas of Tarentum** <note>Archytas was a famous mathematician, statesman, and friend of Plato</note>, at the beginning of his book *On Harmony*, says something similar to this: 

> It seems to me that they <note>The "they" refers to earlier Pythagorean mathematicians</note> have discerned well concerning the mathematical sciences, and it is not at all strange that they think correctly about how each thing is. For having discerned well concerning the nature of the universe, they were bound to see well also concerning the nature of particular things. Indeed, concerning geometry, arithmetic, and spherics, they have handed down to us clear...

The one who has grasped all these things in this manner, as I say—him I call **most wise** <margin>6</margin> and I maintain this both in jest and in earnest." ### NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

...studies which transport our intellect from things of **sense** <note>original: "aisthēta"; objects perceived through the five physical senses.</note> and **opinion** <note>original: "doxasta"; knowledge that is not yet proven or certain.</note> to the things of the **intellect** <note>original: "noēta"; higher realities understood by the mind alone.</note> and **scientific knowledge**. They lead us away from those material and bodily things that have been familiar to us since infancy, toward things that are unfamiliar and alien to our physical senses—yet, by their immateriality and eternity, are more akin to our own souls, and especially to the thinking faculty within them.

<margin>7</margin> Just as Socrates, in Plato's *Republic*, speaks when his conversation partner seems to offer some reasonable justifications for these studies, claiming they are "useful" for human life: **arithmetic** for calculations, distributions, taxes, exchanges, and partnerships; **geometry** for the laying out of military camps, the founding of cities and temples, and the measurement of land; **music** for festivals, joy, and the worship of the gods; and **spherics** <term>Spherics</term> <note>The ancient study of the geometry of the celestial sphere, often considered the theoretical foundation of astronomy.</note> and **astronomy** for agriculture, navigation, and other human activities, as they reveal the proper timing and suitability of actions. <margin>P</margin> <margin>7</margin> Rebuking him, Socrates says: "How charming you are! You seem to be afraid that I might be prescribing these studies as useless. But it is a very difficult thing—or rather, an impossible one—for the **eye of the soul** <note>original: "omma tēs psychēs"; a famous Platonic image for the mind's ability to see truth, which can be blinded by worldly concerns.</note>, [which has been blinded] by other pursuits..."

### INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...pursuits, which is blinded and buried, is by these alone rekindled and awakened; it is better to save this than ten thousand physical eyes, for by it alone is the truth regarding the whole is seen.

<margin>5</margin> **IV.** Which of these four methods, then, is it necessary to learn first? Clearly, it is the one which by nature exists before all the others and holds a more sovereign rank, acting as a beginning and a root and, as it were, fulfilling the role of a mother to the rest. And this is **arithmetic**. <margin>2</margin> 

<margin>10</margin> This is true not only because we said that it pre-existed the others in the mind of the craftsman God <note>original: "τεχνίτου θεοῦ" (technitou theou); referring to the Demiurge or Divine Architect.</note> as a kind of cosmic and exemplary ratio <note>original: "logon"; here meaning a formative principle, plan, or proportion.</note> and model, upon which the **Creator** <term>Demiurge</term> <note>From the Greek "dēmiourgos" (craftsman). In Platonic philosophy, this is the figure who shapes the material world according to eternal patterns.</note> of all things leaned as if on a preliminary design and archetypal pattern to order the products made of matter and make them attain their proper purpose, <margin>15</margin> but also because it is by nature prior in existence, insofar as it cancels the others along with itself if it is removed, but is not itself canceled when they are removed. 

For instance, "living being" is prior to "man" by nature; for if "living being" is removed, "man" is also removed, <margin>20</margin> but if "man" is removed, "living being" is not necessarily canceled along with him. And again, "man" is prior to "grammarian"; for if man does not exist, neither does a grammarian, but if a grammarian does not exist, it is still possible for man to exist. So, since it cancels...

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the argument from page 9, where Nicomachus explains the natural priority of arithmetic. He is defining the logical principle that a "prior" concept is one that can exist without the "posterior," but the posterior cannot exist without the prior.</meta>

# NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

<margin>3</margin> ...and is for this reason called "prior." Conversely, a thing is called "younger" or "posterior" if it implies the existence of the more general term along with itself, but is not itself implied by that term. For example, consider the **"musician"**: it necessarily implies the existence of **"man"** along with it. Or again, consider **"horse"**: <margin>5</margin> the **"living being"** is necessarily implied by it, but the reverse is not true. For if a "living being" exists, it is not necessary that it be a "horse," nor if a "man" exists is it necessary that he be a "musician."

<margin>4</margin> <margin>10</margin> This same principle applies to the aforementioned sciences. If **geometry** exists, it is necessary that **arithmetic** be implied along with it. For at the same time as geometry, there exists the triangle, or the square, or the octahedron, or the icosahedron, <note>These are geometric solids; for instance, the octahedron has 8 faces and the icosahedron has 20.</note> or the <margin>p</margin> double, <margin>8</margin> <margin>15</margin> or the eightfold, or the **sesquialter** <term>sesquialter</term> <note>original: "hēmiolion"; a ratio of 3:2, where one number contains another and half of it besides.</note>, or any other such thing that geometry discusses. Such things cannot be conceived of without the numbers that are implied with each of them. For how is it possible for something to be, or to be called, **"triple"** <margin>20</margin> without the number **3** being pre-supposed, or **"eightfold"** without the **8** being supposed? On the other hand, the numbers 3 and 4 and those that follow could exist even if the figures derived from their names <note>original: "parōnymōn schēmatōn"; referring to shapes like triangles or squares named after numbers.</note> did not exist.

<margin>5</margin> Therefore, arithmetic eliminates geometry along with itself if it is removed, but it is not eliminated by geometry's removal; it is implied by geometry, but does not itself imply geometry.

<margin>V</margin> **V.** Again, consider this in the case of **music**...

<meta>Continuity note: On the previous page, Nicomachus established the principle of logical priority, arguing that arithmetic is the foundation of all other sciences because it can exist without them, while they cannot exist without arithmetic. He illustrated this with the examples of "man" being prior to "musician" and "living being" being prior to "horse."</meta>

# INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...[we have shown] that the absolute is prior by nature to the relative, just as "large" is prior to "larger," "rich" is prior to "richer," and "man" is prior to "father." Furthermore, the musical concords—the fourth, the fifth, and the octave—are named according to number. 
<margin>5</margin> Likewise, they possess their harmonic ratios in strictly numerical terms: the **Fourth** is a **sesquitertian** ratio <note>original: "epitritos"; a ratio of 4:3, where the first number contains the second plus one-third of it.</note>, the **Fifth** is a **sesquialter** ratio <note>original: "hēmiolios"; a ratio of 3:2, where the first number contains the second plus one-half of it.</note>, the **Octave** is a **double** ratio (2:1), 
<margin>10</margin> the **Octave-plus-Fifth** is a **triple** ratio (3:1), and the most perfect concord of all, the **Double-Octave**, is a **quadruple** ratio (4:1).

2. It is even more evident that **Astronomy** <note>original: "hē sphairikē," the science of the spheres.</note> relies upon arithmetic for all its proper investigations. This is not only because it is posterior to geometry (for motion, by its nature, follows after rest), nor merely because the movements of the stars are composed of every harmonious melody, 
<margin>15</margin> but because the risings and settings, the forward-progressions and retrogradations, the occultations <note>original: "epiprosthēseis"; literally "additions," referring to when one celestial body covers another.</note>, and the various phases of the moon are all clearly defined by numerical periods and quantities.

3. Since arithmetic is prior by nature, more honorable, 
<margin>20</margin> and older—serving as a mother and a nurse—we have rightly established its systematic treatment first. For the sake of clarity, we shall begin our instruction from this point.

***

1. **another** <note>original: "allo"</note>] **different** <note>original: "heteron"; found in manuscripts CS</note> — 4. 5. **the** <note>original: "dia"</note>] **the [interval] of** <note>original: "hē dia"; occurs three times in manuscript H</note> — 5. **num-ber** <note>syllable break between G1 and G2</note> — **nominative** <note>original: "onomastikai"; superscript note in manuscript S: "that is, being named"</note> — 7. **they have** <note>manuscripts G1/G2 use the movable 'nu'</note> — 7—9. **sesquitertian ... sesquialter ... double ... triple ... quadruple** <note>variant grammatical cases in manuscripts CSH</note> — 10. **perfection** <note>original: "teleiotēta"; found in manuscript P</note> — 11. **obtains** <note>original: "epitynchanei"; found in manuscript H</note> — 12. **that** <note>original: "hoti"</note>] **since** <note>original: "epeidē"; found in CS</note> — 13. **by nature** <note>original: "physei"</note>] **later** <note>original: "hystera"; added in H</note> — 16. **period** <note>original: "periodon"; singular in P</note> — 18. **phases** <note>omitted in P</note> **pha///seis** <note>erasure in G</note> **phaūseis** <note>spelling in m</note> **phauseis** <note>spelling in S</note> **variations of the moon** <note>Latin: "lunae variationes"; as translated by the Roman philosopher Boethius</note> — 19. **of nature** <note>original: "physeōs"; found in P</note> — **most honorable** <note>original: "timiōtatas"; H; marginal note: "I think 'in honor'"</note> — 21. **the** <note>original: "tēn"</note>] **to it** <note>original: "autē"; H</note> — **systematic treatment** <note>original: "technol."</note>] **of it** <note>original: "autēs"; added in manuscripts C and Γ</note> — **we established** <note>spelling variant in S</note>

<summary>Nicomachus explains how arithmetic provides the fundamental ratios for music (like the Octave and Fifth) and the numerical cycles for astronomy. He concludes that arithmetic is the "mother and nurse" of all sciences and must be studied first.</summary>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues Nicomachus's philosophical justification for the study of arithmetic. Having previously shown that arithmetic is logically prior to other sciences like music and astronomy, he now argues that number is the fundamental "blueprint" used by the creator of the universe.</meta>

# NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

<margin>VI</margin> <margin>6.</margin> All things that have been arranged by nature in the universe according to a skillful progression appear, both in their individual parts and as a whole, to have been distinguished and beautified by Providence and the Mind that created all things. This order was established according to number, for the pattern was pre-established <margin>5</margin> like a preliminary sketch <note>original: "procharagma"; a plan or architectural drawing made before construction begins.</note> through the fact that number existed beforehand in the mind of the God who made the world. This number was purely conceptual <note>original: "noēton"; existing in the intellect rather than the physical world.</note> and entirely immaterial, <margin>P</margin> <margin>9</margin> yet it was the truly eternal essence. It served as a "technical plan" <note>original: "logos technikos"; a rational, structural principle.</note> so that all these things might be brought to completion: <margin>10</margin> time, motion, the heavens, the stars, and their various cycles.

<margin>2</margin> It is therefore necessary that "scientific number," <term>Scientific Number</term> <note>original: "epistēmonikos arithmos"; the theoretical study of number itself as a pure concept, distinct from practical counting.</note> which already exists to govern such things, should be harmonized by itself and not by another. <margin>3</margin> For everything that is harmonized is certainly composed of opposites that actually exist. <margin>15</margin> For things that do not exist cannot be harmonized, nor can things that exist but are identical to one another, nor those that differ but have no rational relationship <note>original: "aloga"; literally "without ratio" or "irrational."</note> to each other. It remains, then, that those things from which a harmony is composed must be both existing and different, and must possess a rational relationship <margin>4</margin> to one another. <margin>20</margin> From such things, therefore, scientific number is also [constituted]...

->VI. Joannes Philoponus 42—51. — Boethius, Arithmetic I. 8.<-

---

<note>The following are scholarly notes comparing different surviving manuscripts of this text.</note>

VI, 1. **All** <note>original: "Panta"</note>: manuscript G2 has this in an erasure (perhaps it was "entirely"?) — 2. **arranged** <note>original: "tetagmena"</note>: manuscript S — 5. **beautified** <note>original: "kosmēsthai"</note>: manuscript P; <summary>Nicomachus explains that number is the divine blueprint of the cosmos, existing as an immaterial "technical plan" in the mind of the creator before the physical world was made. He further argues that all harmony, including that of number, must arise from the union of existing, distinct, but rationally related opposites.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion of "scientific number" from page 12. Nicomachus is transitioning from the philosophical origins of number in the mind of the Creator to a formal definition of number and its primary divisions: even and odd.</meta>

-># INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC<-

...scientific number; for there are in it two primary species which possess the essence of quantity and differ from one another and are not of the same genus: the **odd** and the **even**. These are alternated with one another by a wonderful and divine nature, fitted together inseparably and into a single form, as we shall soon see.

<margin>VII</margin> <margin>7.</margin> **Number** is a limited multitude, or a system of units, or a flow of quantity composed of units. The first division of number is into the **even** and the **odd**. <margin>2</margin> The **even** is that which is capable of being divided into two equal parts without a unit <note>original: "monas"; the number one, considered by Greeks as the "atom" or source of number rather than a number itself.</note> intervening in the middle; and the **odd** is that which is not able to be divided into two equal parts because of the aforementioned intervention of the unit. <margin>3</margin> Now, this is the definition according to common popular opinion. 

But according to the Pythagorean way, an **even number** is that which admits division, by the same operation, into the greatest and the smallest parts—greatest in magnitude <note>original: "pēlikotēti"; referring to the size of the parts (e.g., in 10, the halves are 5, which is the largest possible equal part).</note> and smallest in quantity <note>original: "posotēti"; referring to the number of parts (e.g., 2 is the smallest number of parts a whole can be divided into).</note>—according to a natural reciprocity of these two types. The **odd** is that which is not able to experience this, but is instead cut into two unequal parts. <margin>4</margin> In another way, according to the ancient method, the **even** is that which is able to be divided both into two equal parts and into two unequal parts, except <insert>for the first source of the even, the number two</insert>...

---

<margin>VII</margin> References: John Philoponus 52–61. — Iamblichus page 11 and following. — Theon of Smyrna 3–5. — Boethius Book I, chapter 3. — Scholia on Nicomachus by Nobbe, pages 4, 5.

<summary>Nicomachus defines number as a collection of units and introduces the first two species of number: even and odd. He provides three distinct definitions for "even"—the popular, the Pythagorean, and the ancient—emphasizing the balance and "reciprocity" found in numerical division.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the distinction between even and odd numbers from page 13. Nicomachus is concluding the Pythagorean definitions of parity and beginning to explain the unique status of the "Monad" (the number one) as the source of all number.</meta>

### NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

...of its inherent, primordial two-ness <note>original: "dyados"; the principle of duality or "two-ness" which allows for division.</note>, it admits only that one specific division into equal parts. In whatever way it is cut, it reveals only that one single form of number—equality—without any share of the other. An **odd number**, however, is a number which, in any division whatsoever, is divided into unequal parts, always revealing both forms of number <note>even and odd</note> at the same time, never pure of one another, but always existing together. 

According to a reciprocal definition, an **odd number** is that which differs from an **even number** by a unit <note>original: "monadi"; the number one.</note> in either direction—that is, by being one greater or one less. Conversely, an **even number** is that which differs by a unit in either direction from an **odd number**, being one greater and one less.

<margin>VIII</margin> 8. Every number is half the sum of the two numbers placed on either side of it. Likewise, it is half the sum of those numbers placed two steps away on either side, and even those further beyond them, as far as it is possible to go. The **Monad** <note>the number one</note>, however, is unique: because it does not have two numbers flanking it on both sides, it is half of only the single neighboring number <note>The number 2. In the sequence 1, 2, 3, the number 2 is half of 1+3 (4/2=2), but 1 is only half of 2 (2/2=1).</note>. Therefore, the **Monad** is the natural beginning of all things. 

In the further subdivision of the **even**, one type is the **even-times-even**, another is the **odd-even**, and another is the **even-odd**. These are contrary to one another...

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the classification of even numbers. Nicomachus has just introduced the three sub-categories of the even: the even-times-even, the even-odd, and the odd-even. He is now defining the first of these categories.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...just as the **even-times-even** and the **even-odd** are extremes, the **odd-even** <term>perissartion; a number that can be divided by two more than once but eventually ends in an odd number, like 12 or 20</term> is common to both, acting as a middle ground or mean.

<margin>4</margin> An **even-times-even** number <term>artiakis artios; a power of two, such as 4, 8, 16, or 64</term> is, therefore, one which is itself 
<margin>5</margin> able to be divided into two equal parts according to the nature of its genus, and has each of its own parts likewise capable of being divided in two; and again, in the same way, whichever of the parts within those is divisible into two equal parts, and so on until the division of the successive subdivisions 
<margin>10</margin> <margin>5</margin> reaches the naturally indivisible **unit** <term>monad; the number 1, which the Greeks viewed as the source of all numbers rather than a number itself</term>. For the sake of an example, take the number 64: for half of this is 32, and of this 16, and half of this is 8, and of this 4, and of this 2, and then finally the **unit** is the half of this, which, being naturally indivisible, no longer admits 
<margin>15</margin> <margin>6</margin> being halved. It also follows that, whatever part is taken within it, the designation of its name is entirely "even-times-even-named" <note>original: "artiakis artionymon"</note>, and the same applies to the quantity of the units within it as being "even-times-even-powered" <note>original: "artiakis artiodynamon"</note>, never sharing either of these characteristics with any other genus <note>meaning it never results in an odd number until the very end</note>. 
<margin>20</margin> <margin>7</margin> Is it not for this very reason that it is named **even-times-even**, because it is even itself, and its parts and the parts of its parts are even down to the unit?

<summary>This page defines the 'even-times-even' number (powers of 2) as a number that can be repeatedly halved until it reaches the indivisible unit (1). Nicomachus uses the example of 64 to demonstrate this property of continuous divisibility.</summary>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion from page 15 regarding the properties of "even-times-even" numbers (powers of two). Nicomachus is explaining that these numbers and their fractional parts always retain their character as powers of two, both in their name and their numerical value.</meta>

# NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

<margin>8</margin> ...always possesses these properties both in name and in power; and in another way, every part that it may have is **even-times-even** <margin>P 11</margin> according to its name, and this same part is also **even-times-even** according to its power. The generation of the **even-times-even** <term>artiakis artion; numbers produced by doubling, i.e., 2ⁿ</term>, <margin>5</margin> so that none may escape but all fall under this single rule, would occur as follows: proceeding from the **unit** <term>monad; the number 1</term> as from a <margin>9</margin> root, and advancing by a **double ratio** <term>diplasios logos; a ratio of 2:1</term> unto infinity, however many numbers are produced, these are all **even-times-even**, and it is impossible to find others besides these. For example: <margin>10</margin>

->1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512<-

<margin>10</margin> and so on as far as one likes. Each of the aforementioned numbers has indeed been produced by the constant doubling from the unit, and each is entirely **even-times-even**. Furthermore, every part that it is found to have is entirely eponymous <note>original: "parōnymon"; meaning the part's name (like "an eighth") is derived from a number in the same series</note> <margin>15</margin> with one of the numbers within the series, and there exists in it a collection of units equal in quantity to another of the numbers within the series. This occurs through a **reciprocal proportion** <term>antiperistasis; a mutual exchange or circular compensation</term> and exchange: if the terms of the series <margin>19</margin> doubled from the unit are **even** in number, it is impossible for a single middle term <margin>20</margin> to be found, but there are always two, from which the reciprocal proportion and exchange of parts to powers begins...

<summary>Nicomachus explains how to generate "even-times-even" numbers (powers of two) starting from the number one and doubling indefinitely. He notes that these numbers possess a unique symmetry where their fractional parts always correspond to other numbers within the same doubling sequence.</summary>
<keywords>even-times-even, powers of two, double ratio, monad, reciprocal proportion, mathematical series, Greek arithmetic</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the mathematical demonstration from page 16, where Nicomachus explains how the parts (fractions) of a power of two are themselves named after other powers of two in the same series.</meta>

### ARITHMETIC INTRODUCTION

...powers toward parts will proceed in an orderly fashion, first toward the two terms on either side [of the center], then toward those situated above them on each side, until it arrives at the extremes; the result is that the whole is **reciprocally named** <note>original: "antiparōnymeisthai"; referring to the way a number and its corresponding fraction share a name, such as 8 and an eighth</note> after the **unit** <term>monas; the number 1</term> and the **unit** after the whole.

<margin>5</margin> For instance, if we set **128** as the greatest term, the display of terms up to it will be **even-born** <term>artiogeneis; a sequence containing an even number of terms</term>—for there are eight terms in total—and they will not have a single **mean** <term>mesotēta; a middle term in a series</term>, as that is impossible in an even set; rather, they must necessarily have two: the **8** and the **16**, which will correspond to one another <margin>10</margin> reciprocally. For **16** is an **eighth** of the whole 128, and conversely, **8** is a **sixteenth**.

Proceeding further to each side, **32** is a **fourth** part, while **4** is a **thirty-second** part; and again, **64** is a **half**, while **2** is a **sixty-fourth** part; and finally, at the extremities, the **unit** is a **one-hundred-and-twenty-eighth** part, while the whole, conversely, is **128** <margin>11</margin> [units] according to the **unit**.

But if the display consists of an **odd number of terms** <margin>18</margin>, for example seven, where we have set forth **64**, <margin>19</margin> the **mean** will necessarily be one, according to the nature of odd numbers. <margin>P 12</margin> This mean will correspond to itself because it has no partner, but the terms on either side will always correspond to one another until the reciprocity ends at the extremes. For example, the **unit** will be a **sixty-fourth**...

# BY NICOMACHUS OF GERASA

...and the whole according to the **unit** <term>monas; the number 1</term> is **64**, and half of it is **32**, while a **thirty-second** part is **2**; and a **fourth** part is **16**, while a **sixteenth** part is **4**; and the **eighth** part, without any corresponding term <note>original: "antidiastolēs"; meaning there is no other term to pair with it because it is the middle term</note>, is the **8** itself. 

<margin>12</margin> It also happens that for all these series of terms, when they are added together in a <margin>5</margin> heap, they are equal to the term following them minus one unit, so that necessarily the total summation will always be an **odd number**; for a number that is equal to an **even number** minus one unit is always odd. 

<margin>13</margin> This insight will be useful to us, more than any other, for the construction of **perfect numbers** <term>teleioi arithmoi; numbers equal to the sum of their proper divisors, like 6 or 28</term>. For the sake <margin>10</margin> of an example: for **256**, the terms within it down to the unit, when summed, are equal to it minus one unit; and for **128**, the term immediately below it, all those terms within it are similarly equal to it minus one unit. <margin>15</margin> 

The same always applies to the terms within each successive number: just as the **unit** itself is equal to the term after it (which is **2**) minus one unit; and the first two terms together <note>1 + 2 = 3</note> are equal to the term after them <note>4</note> minus one unit; and the first three terms together <note>1 + 2 + 4 = 7</note> are equal to the next term <note>8</note> minus one unit. You will find that this proceeds <margin>20</margin> <margin>14</margin> without fail to infinity. 

It is also most necessary to remember this: if the number of terms in the proposed **even-times even** <term>artiakis artios; a power of two</term> series is even, then the product of the **extremes** <term>akroi; the first and last numbers in the sequence</term> multiplied by each other...

# INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC
### Page 19

...[the products] being completed will be equal to the product of the middle terms with each other; but if the terms are odd in number, the product of the extremes will be equal to the square of the middle term multiplied by itself. For example, **1** times **128** in an even series of terms is equal to **8** times **16**, and also to **2** times **64**, <margin>5</margin> and again to **4** times **32**, and this continues throughout. But in an odd series of terms, **1** times **64** is equal to **2** times **32**, and this is equal to **4** times **16**, and this again to **8** times **8**—the middle term alone being multiplied by itself.

<margin>IX</margin> <margin>10</margin> **9.** The **even-odd** <term>artioperritos; a number that is even but, when divided by two, results in an odd number</term> is a number which is itself even by genus, yet is specifically distinguished from the previously described **even-times even** <term>artiakis artios; a power of two</term>. It is the kind of number that admits a division into two equal parts according to its general genus <note>i.e., it is even</note>, but each of its parts is immediately incapable of being further cut <margin>P 13</margin> <margin>15</margin> into two equal parts. Examples are:
**6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26,**
and those similar to them. For after each of these is halved, the resulting parts are found to be immediately indivisible by two. It also happens <margin>2</margin> to have this property: every part <note>original: "meros"; meaning a divisor or a fraction</note> it is found to have is "opposite-named" to its power, and every quantity of a part is "opposite-powered" <margin>20</margin> to its name, and this is never in any way... <note>Nicomachus is explaining that if the divisor (the 'name') is even, the quotient (the 'power') is odd, and vice versa. For example, in the number 6, the 'half' (even name) is 3 (odd quantity).</note>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion of "even-odd" numbers (numbers that are even but whose halves are odd, such as 6, 10, 14). The previous page ended by noting that in these numbers, the "name" of a divisor (e.g., "half" or "third") and its "power" (its actual numerical value) are always of opposite types—if the name is even, the value is odd, and vice versa.</meta>

# NICOMACHUS OF GERASA
### INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...[it is impossible for] the power of the part to be of the same genus as its name <note>Nicomachus is explaining that for "even-odd" numbers, a part with an even name (like "the half") will always have an odd value, and an odd-named part (like "the third") will always have an even value.</note>. For example, in the case of the number **18**: the **half** <note>original: "hēmisy"</note>, which is named evenly <note>The "half" is the 2nd part; 2 is an even number.</note>, is **9**, which is odd in its power; on the other hand, the **third**, which is given an odd name <note>The 3rd part; 3 is an odd number.</note>, is **6**, which is even in its power. <margin>5</margin> Conversely, the **sixth** part is **3**, and the **ninth** part is **2**; and the same pattern will be found in all other cases of this kind. Perhaps it also obtained such a name <note>i.e., "even-odd"</note> because, despite being even itself, it immediately encounters odd values <margin>10</margin> in its very first halves.

This class of number is generated by taking the series of numbers starting from unity that differ by a dyad <note>i.e., the sequence of odd numbers: 1, 3, 5, 7, etc.</note> arranged in order as far as you wish, and then multiplying them by a **dyad** <note>2</note>. The numbers produced in this order would be:

->**6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26, 30**<-

<margin>15</margin> and as far as you might wish to proceed. The larger numbers always differ from those immediately smaller than them by a **fourth** <note>4</note>. The reason for this is that their original "gnomons" <term>gnomon; here meaning the generating odd numbers (1, 3, 5...) that "bracket" the squares or series</term>, that is, the odd numbers, exceed one another by a **dyad**, and they were lengthened <note>multiplied</note> by a **dyad** in order to create these numbers; <margin>20</margin> and a dyad multiplied by a dyad produces a fourth. Therefore, in the natural fabric of number, these **even-odd** numbers will be found to be the **fifth** from one another <note>In Greek inclusive counting, 6 to 10 is 6, 7, 8, 9, 10—a total of five numbers.</note>, exceeding each other by four, and bypassing **three** numbers in between...

---

1. **of itself** in manuscript S, **of himself** in C — 3. **named** in G — **but the 9 is odd** in S, **the 9 is odd** in H — 4. **given a name** in G — 5. **power** in Gm (H in margin), **substance** in PCSH — **6** in G₁ **sixth** in G₂ — **by inversion** in mC, **by turning back** in P, **from inversion** in S — 6. **9** in G₁ **ninth** in G₂ — 7. **perhaps then** in S — 12. **dyad** in G — **multiplied** in CS — 15. **you might wish** in C, **you wish** in S — 16. **near]** a marginal note adds: "a comment of an anonymous source: 'it is necessary' etc." following the Scholia of John Philoponus 84. — 18. **exceeding** in C — 19. **lengthened** in G, the 'i' changed to 'ē' by the second scribe — **become** in P, **are generated** in S — 23. **three]** **triad** in PH — **but by a dyad]** "but" is omitted in G.

### INTRODUCTION TO ARITHMETIC

...[the series of even-odd numbers] being generated from the sequence of odd numbers. <margin>6</margin> They are said to have properties opposite to those of the **even-times-even** <term>even-times-even: numbers like 2, 4, 8, 16, which can be divided by 2 repeatedly until unity is reached</term>, because in the latter, only the greatest term was divisible <note>In an even-times-even series like 1, 2, 4, 8, only 8 is not a "half" of a larger number in that specific sequence, but more importantly, Nicomachus is contrasting their divisibility limits.</note>, while in the former, only the smallest was indivisible; and furthermore, because <margin>5</margin> in those [even-times-even], the reciprocal relationship of the parts from the extremes to the middle (or middles) resulted in the **product of the extremes** <note>original: "to hypo-" (the rectangle formed by)</note> being equal to the **square of the mean** <note>original: "tō apo-" (the square from)</note> or to the product of the two means; but in these [even-odd numbers], under the same exchange and examination, the mean is **half the sum** of the two extremes added together, or if there should be two means, <margin>10</margin> both together are equal to the sum of the two extremes.

<margin>X</margin> 10. The **odd-even** <note>original: "perissartios"</note> number is that which displays the third <insert>X</insert> <margin>P</margin> form of the **even**, being common to both <margin>14</margin> of those previously mentioned, as if it were itself a certain middle ground between two extremes; for in one respect it is similar to the **even-times-even** <margin>15</margin>, and in another to the **even-odd**. In the respect that it is distinguished from the one, it shares with the other; and in the respect that it has something in common with the one, it differs from the other. It occurs whenever an even number... <margin>2</margin>

---

X. John Philoponus 89–95. — Iamblichus pages 31–35. — Theon 10. — Boethius Book I, chapter 8. — Scholion 53, Nobbe page 6, 7.

1. **procreated** (manuscript C reading; Latin "procreati" in Boethius) — 2. **even-times-even** (variant spelling in manuscripts G and P) — 4. **was** (omitted in manuscripts S and H) — 6. 7. **the product or the square... the square equal to the square of the greater or the product of the mean** (various manuscript corruptions in G, C, and P; it should be understood as: "the product of the extremes is equal to the square of the mean or the product of the means") — 7. **of these** (manuscripts G, H, and S add "in the case of the even-odd numbers") — 8. **examination** (variant in G, C, and H) — 9. **if** (omitted in G and M) — **or also two are** (reading in manuscript P) — 10. **two** (manuscript H)
